The adversarial verification of the D-09 groundwork challenged the premise, I had accepted it from my own earlier probe, and the template settles it: `edit.blade.php:606-616` emits a **hidden `for_variation_N` of 0 PLUS a checkbox of 1**, so the key is always submitted. **THE GATE IS DECIDED BY THE VALUE, NOT THE PRESENCE** — and the value comes from `products.product_attribute`, a JSON column holding `{"for_variation_1":"0"}` per attribute, which renders the checkbox checked or not. **SO THERE ARE TWO BEHAVIOURS, SELECTED BY STORED DATA, and only one of them is this row:** a product stored `for_variation = 0` posts `0` and its variant edits are discarded, which is F82 as measured; a product stored `for_variation = 1` posts the name **twice** (`0` then `1`, PHP keeps the last, so the gate reads **1**) and **the rebuild runs on every ordinary save** — which is far worse and is recorded as **F105**. The earlier probe reported the key absent because it matched only the FIRST occurrence of a repeated multipart name; reading every occurrence is what resolved it. The characterisation tests remain correct — they drive the payloads directly and do not depend on what the page emits — but the ROW's explanation of *why* the gate was closed was wrong, and an implementation built on it would have fixed the wrong thing. **FIXED [2026-10-26] by the D-09 change — and this row's own diagnosis was WRONG and is corrected here.** It said the edit page posts neither gate's keys. It posts all three: the page calls `attribute_option_data()` from `$(document).ready`, which injects the values panel — carrying `options_datas[]` — into the form before the merchant touches anything. Driving the real form produced **154 named parts carrying all three keys**; the original probe matched only the FIRST occurrence of a repeated multipart name and so missed them. So the edit was not discarded for want of a gate key; it was overwritten by a destructive writer. A price typed into the panel now persists, asserted by `ProductVariantOrdinarySaveGuardTest::test_a_price_typed_into_the_values_panel_persists_without_disturbing_the_rest`. A payload carrying NO gate keys is still inert, which is the safe answer for a request that named no combination set, and is still pinned by `ProductVariantPersistenceTest`. |
| **F83** | **The only path that DOES persist a variant edit rebuilds the variant set, and the rebuild HARD-DELETES every variant absent from the posted options and overwrites prices with 0.** Driven end to end in the browser on the only persisting path (touch the attribute picker → tick "use for variation" → re-select the terms → edit a price → save): **8 variants before, 4 after** — four deleted outright — and of the four survivors only the edited one kept a price, the other three were written to `0` from `NULL`. Two mechanisms, both in code: `ProductVariant::where('product_id', …)->whereNotIn('variant', $com)->delete()` at `:2021` (and the sibling at `:1874`) removes every variant not in the posted set, and a raw `DB::table` read cannot see them either, so it is a hard delete, not a soft one; and every field is read as `!empty($request[…]) ? … : 0`, so a **covered** variant whose price field is absent or blank is zeroed rather than left alone. | **CHARACTERISED, NOT FIXED — same decision point A.** This is the reason F82's obvious fix is not safe. Three driven tests plus a static ratchet asserting exactly two `whereNotIn` rebuild deletes and one unconditional one; mutation-checked both ways (a third delete, and a changed predicate, each fail it). The driven tests are differential by construction — the same payload with and without the gate keys produces opposite outcomes — so none can pass vacuously. **[2026-10-26] REACHABILITY CORRECTED, AND IT IS WORSE THAN RECORDED.** This row described the rebuild as "the only path that DOES persist", reachable when a merchant deliberately re-selects the attribute configuration. Measured: for a product whose `products.product_attribute` marks an attribute as used for variations, **the rebuild runs on an ordinary save with no merchant interaction whatsoever** — see F105. So the delete and the zeroing are not behind a deliberate act; they are behind stored data. **FIXED [2026-10-26] by the D-09 change.** The zeroing and the delete are separate halves and each got its own treatment. The zeroing came from `!empty($request[k]) ? $request[k] : DEFAULT` on every per-variant column: the panel echoes a BLANK for any column that is NULL, so an ordinary save re-posted blanks and the writer wrote the defaults. `ssVariantValue()` replaces it — an absent or blank key leaves an existing column alone and only takes the default on a row being created, and a posted `"0"` is now stored as 0, which `!empty("0") === false` made impossible before. The hard delete is now gated on the posted combination set actually differing from the rows that exist, so a deliberate narrowing still deletes (pinned by a premise test) and an ordinary save cannot. |

Found by the `__()`-coverage sweep: it is the one string on a Phase 02 surface with no Arabic translation. | **BLOCKED on owner decision point C — the owner supplies the text.** Every available action states a refund rule: translating it publishes a policy that is not Store Syria's, in Arabic, on every receipt; rewriting it means authoring the real policy; deleting the paragraph leaves the receipt with none. Directly covered by the owner's instruction *"ولا تغيّر قواعد التسوية أو رد الأموال دون ضرورة مثبتة ومراجعة مستقلة"* and *"لا تخترع سلوكاً مالياً من عندك"*. | **F86** | **Arabic — the platform's PRIMARY language — had no `validation.php`, so every built-in validation message was English, on every form in the product.** Measured on the live edit page with `lang=ar`: an empty name rendered `"The name field is required."` at the field, while the five hand-written custom messages in `ProductController` rendered in Arabic. The split is the source: custom messages go through `__()` and resolve from `ar.json`; Laravel's built-in rule messages resolve from `lang/<locale>/validation.php`, and `resources/lang/ar/` held only `installer_messages.php` and `myfatoorah.php`. `he`, `zh` and `pt-br` all ship one; Arabic did not. A second defect in the same place: `:attribute` resolved to the raw **column** name, so the Arabic sentence read `"الحقل name مطلوب."`. **And a third, from the same sweep:** 16 `__()` strings on Phase 02 merchant surfaces had no `ar.json` key, **four of them added by earlier Phase 02 slices of this project** — work done under a bilingual rule had shipped English-only four times before anything measured it. | **FIXED [2026-10-26], additively.** `resources/lang/ar/validation.php` (106 messages, every placeholder preserved) plus `auth.php`, `passwords.php`, `pagination.php`, which were missing for the same reason; 15 keys added to `ar.json`; and the Validator's **fourth** argument (`ssProductFieldNames()`) at both call sites rather than a global `validation.attributes` map, which would rename the field in every message on every form in the platform. Now reads `"الحقل الاسم مطلوب."` / `"The Name field is required."` and `"يجب أن تكون قيمة السعر 0 على الأقل."` / `"The Price must be at least 0."`. Driven **93 pass / 0 fail**, seven cases × two languages. Two ratchets: `ArabicValidationMessagesTest` (14) compares the locale files key-for-key **and placeholder-set-for-placeholder-set**, because a dropped `:min` renders a message with its value missing and the live probe checks the opposite failure; `ArabicUiCoverageTest` (3) keeps the `__()` set closed per surface. **Both revert-checked, and the second one's premise had to be strengthened:** a whole-scan key-count floor passed while a third of the surface area went unscanned (deleting the `product/` glob left 227 keys, clearing a 200 floor), so the premise is now per-surface. **One suspicion was REFUTED rather than reported:** a negative `price` and a `cost_price` above `price` were accepted on product 1 — not a missing rule, but the `variant_product == 1` branch omitting the price rules on purpose, because a variant product's prices live on `product_variants` and the parent `price` is only written inside `if ($request->variant_product == 0)` (`:1643`). The probe had reached the wrong branch; re-driven against a simple product, every price rule fires. |
| **F87** | **A payment provider classified as "provider verified" gates the API ENVELOPE, not the payment — so a failed or abandoned transaction activates the plan, repeatably.** `PaystackPaymentController.php:150` is the only gate: `if (isset($result['status']) && $result['status'] == true)`. That is whether Paystack's verify *call* succeeded. The handler then records the real transaction state three lines into the body — `$order->payment_status = $result['data']['status'];` (`:183`) — so it demonstrably knows where the payment state lives, and **nothing requires it to be `success`**. Verified by listing every `if (` between the gate and `$user->assignPlan($plan->id)` at `:204`: only coupon bookkeeping and the result of `assignPlan` itself. The amount is never compared to the plan price either, just recorded as `$result['data']['amount'] / 100`. Three aggravating facts: **no kill switch** (the controller does not use `GuardsPlanActivation`, so the 403 protecting 26 other providers does not apply — and it is on the *verified* list); **the reference is caller-chosen** (`{pay_id}` is a route parameter, and the plan is `Crypt::decrypt()`, which CLAUDE.md records as an encoding rather than a control because `APP_KEY` is in git history); and **no replay guard**, while the project owns one (`GuardsPlanActivation::existingPlanOrderForProviderTxn()`) that this handler does not call. Of the **18** providers on the verified list, 17 markers describe a check on the payment state (`'PAID'`, `'COMPLETED'`, `paid == 1 && captured == 1`, `isSuccessful()`, `isPaid()`, …) and two were checked against their code and match exactly; Paystack is the single outlier. | **VERIFIED, NOT FIXED — BLOCKED on decision point E (D-12).** Plan activation is **Phase 06**, which the owner's scope precedence keeps out of Phase 02, and each candidate fix is a decision rather than a correction: requiring `data.status === 'success'` is a one-line change that is almost certainly right but *is* a payment-gate behaviour change; moving Paystack onto the existing fail-closed map needs no new code but makes Paystack plan purchases return 403, which breaks the flow for any merchant actually buying through it. **Confidence is split and stated that way:** *certain* that the handler never tests `data.status`, never compares the amount and has no activation or replay guard — that is structural and readable; *not executed* that Paystack's envelope really is `true` for a failed transaction, which is its documented contract but was not driven from here. The structural half suffices: a gate that ignores the field the handler itself records as the payment status is wrong whatever the provider returns. **FIXED [2026-10-26] — owner decision D-17, as an isolated security fix.** The gate now requires `$result['data']['status'] === 'success'` **and** that `data.reference` equals the reference this server asked about; a replay is refused by `existingPlanOrderForProviderTxn()`, which the handler now reaches by adopting `GuardsPlanActivation`. **`assertPlanActivationEnabled()` is deliberately NOT called**: Paystack has no entry in `config/payments.php`'s `plan_activation` map and that helper treats a missing key as disabled, so calling it would `abort(403)` on every Paystack plan purchase. D-17 reserves the fail-closed route for the case where verification safety cannot be proven; here it can be. **THE AMOUNT IS LOGGED, NOT REFUSED, and that is deliberate.** `GuardsPlanActivation`'s own docblock records the reason — "a verified provider may legitimately post an amount back in minor units, and a blind comparison would refuse real payments on a live integration". This repo *believes* Paystack reports minor units because it divides by 100, but that is read off the code, not measured against a live response, and no Paystack account is reachable from here. A disagreement with `serverOwnedPlanPrice()` is logged at warning and the provider's captured figure is still recorded, because that is what was charged. **Making it a refusal needs one real Paystack response to settle the unit.** **THE DECISION IS A SEPARATE, PURE METHOD** (`paystackVerificationOutcome()`) because the handler reaches Paystack with a raw `curl_init()`, not the HTTP client — there is nothing to fake, and a route-level test in this container would have passed against the defect, since a failed curl refuses on the old code too. `PaystackPlanActivationTest` drives failure, cancellation, success, replay, a mismatched reference and an unusable response; **four of its nine fail when the gate is mutated back**. | **ONE MORE THING THE FIX HAD TO GET RIGHT, found by re-reading my own diff rather than by a test.** The replay branch originally returned "Plan activated Successfully" to **whoever** presented a used reference. Scoping the LOOKUP by user would have been actively dangerous — a second account would then find nothing, fall through, and have its own plan activated on a stranger's payment — so the lookup stays global and only the RESPONSE depends on the owner: the buyer re-opening their own return URL gets the success they expect, anyone else is refused and the attempt is logged. Otherwise the handler confirms to a stranger that a guessed reference is a real payment, and the reference is a route parameter. **The remaining window is F119:** no unique index backs the guard. **THE AMOUNT IS NOW A REFUSAL, NOT A LOG — see F120**, which records why the first version of this fix got that wrong and what refuted it. Two further defects in my own fix were found by the same review and corrected in the same change: the replay guard checked only the CURRENT LOCALE's spelling of `payment_type`, and the locale is the caller's to choose through the language switcher, so a merchant could step around a row written in another language — it now checks every shipped locale; and the replay branch reported success without checking the recorded PLAN, and without re-running the activation, so a request that died between the `PlanOrder` insert and `assignPlan()` would have stranded a real payer permanently, every later retry being told the plan was active while it was not. **That failure had a reachable trigger today:** `?coupon=` with no `coupon_id` dereferences a null and raises an `Error`, which `catch (\Exception)` does not catch — after the row is written. |
| **F88** | **The posture test asserts that a verification marker EXISTS, not that it is adequate — which is why F87 survived a dedicated security pass.** `PlanPaymentPostureTest::test_every_verified_provider_is_recorded_with_its_verification_marker()` requires each provider on the verified list to carry a marker. Paystack carries one, and the marker **accurately describes an inadequate check**: `"outbound GET api.paystack.co/transaction/verify/{ref} then $result['status'] == true"`. ''` null guard alone does **not**, so that guard is hardening this suite does not exercise; **(2) the sibling writers are NOT affected, and a bare grep says otherwise.** CinetPay, Easebuzz, AuthorizeNet and Khalti read `$product->variant_id` too, but their basket is `(array) $cartlist_response->getData()->data` and that cast is **shallow**, so `product_list` stays an array of stdClass and object syntax is correct for them. The sweep missed this instance because the spelling differed: the three it fixed were missing array keys, this one is object syntax on an array. It would also fatal on first use regardless: `Coupon`, `User`, `Setting`, `Product`, `ProductVariant`, `ApiController` and `CartController` are all referenced unqualified and **none is imported**, so they resolve to `App\Http\Controllers\*` and do not exist. Deletion was already the conclusion reached in `docs/development/phase-1/11-NEXT-PACKAGE-PRODUCTVARIANT-TENANCY.md:197`; this review confirms the code is unchanged. |
| **F95** | **The two anonymous routes that WRITE AN ORDER and DECREMENT STOCK are unthrottled, while the harmless route declared immediately above them is throttled.** `routes/customer.php:183` `POST {storeSlug?}/get-massage` carries `->middleware('throttle:storefront-relay')` and only builds a WhatsApp URL — it writes nothing. `:184` `POST {storeSlug?}/telegram` and `:185` `POST {storeSlug?}/whatsapp` both reach `PaymentController::whatsapp` (`:3558`), which writes an order at `:4112` and decrements stock in its cart loop, and **neither carries any throttle**. The handler has **no replay guard of any kind** — verified by reading all 903 lines of it: no `claimGatewayReturn`, no `Cache::lock`, no `Cache::add`, no lookup of an existing order. The only thing stopping a sequential resubmit is incidental: the cart rows were deleted after the first commit, so the second attempt answers `'Cart is empty.'` — and two *concurrent* posts both read a non-empty cart before either delete runs, giving two orders and two stock decrements. | **REPORTED, NOT CHANGED — and most of what looks alarming here is DECIDED DESIGN, which is the point of writing it up carefully.** Three things are deliberate and already adversarially reviewed, so they are **not** findings: the `$request->all()` fallback exists because WhatsApp/Telegram intake must accept a post with no prior session (so a nonce-based replay guard **cannot** exist on this path); the route slug beats the body's; the caller's `shipping_final_price` is zeroed on the fallback arm; and ownership comes from `auth('customers')` or the server's stored state, never the body. And a whatsapp order being **`Paid`** is an **owner decision** — `PaymentVerificationService::OFFLINE_PAYMENT_TYPES` means "settles off-platform by arrangement, so record Paid at once", governed by D-06 (2026-10-22), whose own comment is explicit that the no-unverified-Paid rule covers **gateway** payments and an off-gateway arrangement is not one. **So the finding is narrow and real: an anonymous, unthrottled, un-deduplicated order intake whose orders are recorded Paid and whose stock moves.** The asymmetry with `:183` is the evidence — the throttle was put on the neighbour that writes nothing. **Why not fixed:** the change is one `->middleware('throttle:...')`, and named limiters already exist (`auth`, `order-track`, `storefront-relay`), so the machinery is there — but choosing the rate needs real traffic data this environment does not have, and a limit set too low returns 429 on a legitimate checkout. The owner sets the rate; the one-line change is stated so it needs no re-derivation. |
| **F96** | **The Paymera callback's idempotency lock has a 10-second TTL and is held across SMTP and SMS calls, so a slow mail server reopens the duplicate-order window the lock exists to close.** `PaymentController.php:620-623` takes `Cache::lock('paymera_lock_' . $orderId, 10)`; the order writer `getProductStatus()` is called **inside** it; `$pending['processed'] = true` is not written until `:725`, **after** that writer returns; the lock is released at `:738`. And the writer's stock loop makes **external** calls — `Utility::sendEmailTemplate()` at `:2607` and `:2665`, `Utility::SendMsgs()` at `:2615` and `:2673`. An SMTP or SMS timeout alone can exceed ten seconds. If the writer runs long the lock **lapses while `processed` is still false**, a concurrent replay acquires it, `freshPaymeraPendingOrNull()` (`:792`) still reads `processed = false`, and a second order is written for one payment. **The project's own reasoning establishes the premise, one layer down:** the comment at `:2878-2886` says the database transaction was deliberately placed OUTSIDE that loop because *"holding row locks across an external call is the mistake this boundary exists to avoid"*. The cache lock is held across exactly those calls. | **RECORDED, NOT CHANGED — and it is a window, not an open door.** Paymera's callback is otherwise the **best-guarded** path in the codebase and the reference the rest of E14 measures against: a `processed` pre-check at `:535`, the lock, a **re-check inside the lock**, fail-closed status polling, amount and signature verification before the order is created, and `OrderCompleteBinding` so a replay cannot disclose the buyer's order to a stranger (P1-ORD-13). This finding narrows to the TTL versus the writer's worst-case duration. **Candidate fixes, each a decision:** raise the TTL (picks a number without traffic data); set `processed = true` BEFORE calling the writer and clear it on failure (changes the failure semantics of a payment path); or move the notification I/O out of the stock loop (changes a payment path's behaviour). All three are reserved, so the finding is reported with the evidence. **It is also NOT DRIVEN, and the reason is worth stating:** `Cache::lock` on the `array` driver tests run under is process-local, so no Feature test can stage this interleave — proving it needs MySQL plus `CACHE_STORE=database`, which is the `tests/Support/Security/bin/verify-*-on-mysql.php` pattern this project already uses for evidence that cannot live in the suite. Claimed as a structural window, not as a measured duplication. **TRIAGED [2026-10-26] — ITS OWN SLICE, and the obvious patches are all wrong.** Raising the 10-second TTL does not fix it: no TTL is long enough if the writer is waiting on SMTP. Writing `processed = true` before the writer runs is worse — a crash mid-write would then leave a payment marked processed with no order. The correct fix is the one the codebase already states one layer down at `PaymentController:2878-2886`: do not hold the lock across external calls, i.e. move the mail and SMS sends out of the writer's stock loop. That is a restructuring of the order writer's notification path, with its own tests, and it touches every gateway that shares the writer. **Not urgent in the queue sense:** it is a window that needs a replay to arrive while an SMTP call is hanging, on the best-guarded path in the codebase. |
| **F97** | **A gateway's server-to-server trigger route sits behind `auth`, so the gateway can never reach it — and the code's own comment asserts the opposite.** `PaymentController.php:1055` states *"This caller is the GATEWAY, not the buyer: the route is anonymous and CSRF-exempt"* and the handler's P1-ORD-13 reasoning is built on that premise. Measured: `POST store/paymera/trigger/{orderId}` is declared at `routes/web.php:514`, which is **inside** the `Route::middleware(['auth','xss','setlocate','verified'])` group opened at `:400` and closed at `:1434` — confirmed by reading both boundaries and by `route:list`. The CSRF half of the claim is right (`bootstrap/app.php` exempts `store/paymera/trigger/*`); the **anonymous** half is wrong. So Paymera's server-to-server poke arrives unauthenticated, is redirected to `/login`, and **never reaches the handler**. The anonymous `GET store/paymera/callback/{orderId}` is the only live Paymera entry point. | **RECORDED, NOT CHANGED — two defects in one, and the functional half is the bigger one.** As documentation it is the **F93** family: a comment that inverts the truth in exactly the place a reviewer looks to decide whether a path is safe, and here it also underpins a security argument (the handler's anonymous-caller reasoning is correct *as written* but defends a door nobody can open). As behaviour it means one of the two routed Paymera entry points is **inert for its intended caller**, so the gateway's confirmation arrives only if the buyer's browser completes the GET callback — there is no server-to-server fallback when the buyer closes the tab. **Not changed because moving a payment route out of the `auth` group changes its exposure from authenticated to anonymous**, which is precisely the kind of payment-path change the standing limits reserve — and the correct remedy may instead be a signed route or an allowlist rather than plain anonymity. Carried on **D-13** with the other gateway-posture decisions. **The method note worth keeping:** this was found by checking prose against `route:list` rather than against the route file alone; the declaration's indentation gives no hint that it is 900 lines inside a middleware group. **TRIAGED AND HALF-FIXED [2026-10-26].** The two halves separate cleanly. **The documentation half is fixed now**, under the same authority as F93: the comment in `handlePaymeraTrigger` asserted the route was anonymous, and `route:list -v` measures `POST store/paymera/trigger/{orderId}` carrying `web, Authenticate, XSS, SetLocate, EmailIsVerified` against the callback's bare `web`. The comment now states the measurement, keeps the P1-ORD-13 reasoning (it is correct as written and becomes load-bearing the moment the route moves), and `PaymeraCallbackIdempotencyTest::test_the_paymera_trigger_route_is_still_behind_auth` pins it from the **booted router** in both directions — the trigger must keep `auth`, the callback must never gain it — so the comment cannot drift back and a deliberate move fails the test and names what to revisit. **The functional half stays an owner decision:** moving a payment route out of the `auth` group changes a payment route's authentication. Until it is taken, Paymera has **no server-to-server fallback** — confirmation arrives only if the buyer's browser completes the GET callback. |

'</span><br></a>' . $customer->mobile` — and **`email` had no `editColumn` at all**, so the raw model value was emitted. All four values are written by a shopper: `CustomerLoginController::register` validates `first_name` as exactly `['required','string','max:255']` and nothing more, so a shopper of the merchant's own store chooses the string. **The XSS middleware does not cover it:** `app/Http/Middleware/XSS.php` runs `htmlspecialchars_decode()` and THEN strips only `&lt;`, `&gt;`, `javascript`, `alert` — the decode turns an encoded `&lt;` into a raw `<` before the strip looks for `&lt;`, and a raw `<` was never a target, so `<img src=x onerror=…>` passes through whole as long as it avoids two words. **`CustomerDataTable` had no escaping call of any kind** — measured: zero occurrences of `e(` or `htmlspecialchars`. | **FIXED [2026-10-26], driven and revert-checked.** `e()` on each of `first_name`, `last_name` and `mobile`, and **`email` REMOVED from `rawColumns()` rather than escaped** — nothing in that cell is ever markup, so the right answer is to stop declaring it raw and let yajra escape it, with no closure needed. `tests/Feature/Security/CustomerListOutputEscapingTest.php` (6 tests) drives all four fields and pins both halves: absence of the raw tag **and** presence of the escaped form, because absence alone would also pass if the column stopped rendering. The loop is inside no `try`. **Worse placed than F89:** these sit after `chargeSale()` captured (`:327`) and after the stock decrement (`:773`), but **before `new Order()`** — so the card is debited, the stock is gone, **there is no order row at all**, and the checkout claim is already consumed so the retry is refused too. Reproduced as **HTTP 500 with the capture recorded** as a fifth case on `test_a_charged_card_does_not_end_in_a_crash`, then fixed, then revert-checked. **`(object) $product` rather than a different argument**, because that is behaviour-identical to what these sites passed before the migration: the helpers read exactly `->product_id` and `->name`, and the cart line carries both keys, so the cast restores the pre-migration object with the same two values and changes nothing downstream. Passing a loaded `Product` model instead would make `->product_id` null and lean on the helpers' `!empty()` fallback — a different value reaching a notification message, for no benefit. **ONE HONEST LIMIT, measured not assumed:** reverting the first cast alone fails the new case, so **only that one of the four is test-covered**; the other three need a different stock figure and notification flag each. written from `Api\ApiController:8197` as `$request->email`) and `TestimonialDataTable` (username — a **shopper** writes the review, `Api\ApiController:8293`) are shopper→merchant, the same direction as F98 and squarely Phase 02; The actions are now the DS's own quiet icon buttons (`ss-btn ss-btn--sm ss-btn--icon ss-btn--ghost` / `--secondary` / `--danger-quiet`), each with an `aria-label` that names the **customer** as well as the verb, and `aria-hidden="true"` on every icon. `title` and the tooltip stay — a sighted affordance worth keeping, and where both exist `aria-label` is what assistive technology announces. **The delete conversion was driven end to end rather than assumed**, because a styling change that silently breaks delete would be worse than the inconsistency it fixed: a separate probe asserts SweetAlert and jQuery are present on the page, that the button is inside its form with a CSRF token and a spoofed DELETE, that clicking opens the **project** dialog and not a raw `confirm()`, and that confirming really issues `POST /customer/{id}` and is accepted — 13/13. **A false failure on the way is worth recording:** the first version of that probe asserted on the row count and reported a working delete as broken, because `tbody tr` on this page is 5 while there are 3 customer rows; and `el.click()` inside `page.evaluate` does not drive SweetAlert's own handler. The request is what is being tested, so the request is what it measures now. Nine strings were translated as part of this (five new, four pre-existing on the same screen). |

A premise that over-counts invents defects exactly as readily as one that under-counts invents passes. | **BOTH FIXED [2026-10-26].** Real data rows are now collected from **visible** tables only (`offsetParent !== null`, which is null inside a `display:none` subtree) with the empty-notice row filtered out, and `emptyNotice` is reported separately so the premise **refuses an empty list outright** and skips the remaining checks with a visible SKIP rather than measuring nothing. After both fixes: orders, products and customers, four configurations each, **148 cases, 148 passed, 0 failed**. **And a third, recorded because it has now bitten this project four times:** the probe is a **template literal**, so the backticks in the explanatory comment I added inside it terminated the string and the file stopped parsing (`SyntaxError: Unexpected identifier 'table'`). The comment is now backtick-free and says so at the point where someone would reintroduce it. |
| **F104** | **The stock reports and the product list gave three different answers about one product, because each had its own low-stock predicate.** The product list's rule is `ProductDataTable::trackedStockState()`, shared with the list's own filter so the badge and the filter cannot disagree (P2-02-3, P2-03-9): a per-product `low_stock_threshold` of **0 means UNSET** and falls back to the store's `out_of_stock_threshold`, and a tracked product is `out` only at **zero**. Neither report used it. `ReportController::getLowStockProducts` compared `product_stock <= low_stock_threshold` with **no fallback**, so an unset threshold could never be low there. `getOutOfStockProducts` used the store's `out_of_stock_threshold` as the **out** boundary — reading a low-stock fallback as an out-of-stock level. Two divergences in **opposite** directions, which is why a single product exhibits all three at once: with `low_stock_threshold = 0`, `product_stock = 3` and a store threshold of 5, the product list says **Low stock**, the low-stock report **omits it** (3 <= 0 is false), and the out-of-stock report **lists it as out**. The merchant reads one thing on the catalogue, cannot find the product in the report meant for exactly that, and finds it called out of stock in the next tab. | **FIXED [2026-10-26], TDD and revert-checked per half.** Both reports, on both their non-variant and variant legs, now call `trackedStockState()` and test its verdict — `=== 'low'` and `=== 'out'` — instead of carrying their own comparison. `StockReportPredicateTest` (5 tests) drives the real report endpoints and compares membership against **`trackedStockState()` called directly**, not against a restated copy of the rule: a test that restated it would pass while both sides were wrong together, which is the failure this slice is about. Four of the five cases **pass against the unfixed code** and exist as guard rails so a fix cannot simply widen both reports — a healthy product in neither, a zero-stock product in the out report only, a product whose **own** threshold is above the store's still low, and an **untracked** product in neither (the list makes no level claim for one, so the reports may not invent one). Revert-checked in four directions: reverting either predicate fails it, and forcing the low fallback to 0 fails it. **ONE HONEST FINDING FROM THE FOURTH MUTATION:** forcing the OUT report's fallback to 99 changes **nothing** — `trackedStockState()` returns `'out'` on `$stock <= 0` alone, so the threshold only moves the `'low'` boundary and the third argument cannot affect an out verdict. It is still passed, for one shared call shape across both reports, and the code now says so explicitly so that nobody later answers an out-of-stock complaint by tuning that setting. **A hazard closed in the same line that created it:** the low report had no fallback at all, so it had no P1-SEC-26 bug to fix; introducing the fallback introduces it, and `getAdminAllSetting(null, getCurrentStore())` resolves the threshold from the same store as the products rather than from the caller — which for a staff member would walk up to `created_by` and read the **owner's** current store. |
| **F105** | **An ordinary product save, with no field touched, zeroed 11 of 12 variant prices — and this is the corrected, live form of F82/F83.** Driven end to end in the browser. `products.product_attribute` holds a per-attribute JSON flag (`{"attribute_id":"1","for_variation_1":"0"}`). When it reads `"1"`, `edit.blade.php:606-616` renders the "Used for variations" checkbox **checked**, and because the template emits a hidden `for_variation_N=0` **and** a checkbox `=1` under the same name, the POST carries the name **twice** — `0` then `1` — and **PHP keeps the last**. So `ProductController.php:908`'s `if ($for_variation == 1)` reads **1** and the rebuild gate **opens on every ordinary save**. The loop then upserts every posted combination and writes `variation_price = !empty($request[...]) ? ... : 0`, and the variant panels post blank prices for untouched variants. **MEASURED: 12 variants in, 12 out, 0 deleted, and 11 of 12 prices written to 0** — from a save where not one field was touched. The delete did not fire only because `options_datas` happened to cover all 12; it fires whenever the posted option set is narrower than the stored variant set. | **MEASURED, NOT FIXED — and it makes the owner's D-09 instruction (D-16) both correct and more urgent than when he gave it.** His condition is exactly this: *"يُمنع أن يؤدي الحفظ العادي إلى حذف متغيرات غير مرسلة أو تصفير قيم مالية لم يقصد التاجر تعديلها"* — an ordinary save must not delete unsent variants or zero financial values the merchant did not intend to change. It does both today, and which one depends on stored data rather than on anything the merchant does. **THE FIX IS A1 AS INSTRUCTED, and this measurement sharpens what "keep the configuration path separate" has to mean:** it is not enough to add an in-place write path, because the ordinary save would still open the rebuild. The values panel must post to its own endpoint **and** the ordinary product save must stop carrying the rebuild's trigger for a product whose configuration is unchanged. **HOW IT WAS FOUND, because the method matters:** the adversarial verification of the D-09 groundwork refuted the handed-down premise that the gate keys are not posted; re-measuring showed all three present; and reading **every** occurrence of a repeated multipart name — rather than the first, which is what my earlier probe did — showed the gate reading 1. Three rounds of being wrong about one request, each caught by measuring rather than by reasoning. **The dev fixture was corrupted by this probe and the seeder could not restore it** (prices stayed at 0, four stray single-dimension variants left behind); `seed-p2dev.php` now restores mutated variant rows and prunes undeclared ones, the same F102 lesson a second time. **FIXED [2026-10-26] — owner decision D-09, design A1, with the owner's binding additional condition that the ordinary save must stop opening the rebuild at all.** **THE FIX IS THE WRITER, AND TWO GUARDS THAT LOOKED RIGHT WERE BUILT, REVIEWED AND REMOVED — F113.** The natural design — compare the posted attribute configuration against the stored `products.product_attribute` — was drafted, adversarially reviewed and **refuted**. That column holds at least four incompatible shapes: `store()` writes the terms verbatim (:305, :621), `update()` writes resolved option ids (:1719, :1812), the degenerate `[""]`, and the literal string `'NULL'` that `edit.blade.php:531` already tests for; and `product_attribute_options.terms` is an unconstrained varchar, so a numeric term cannot be told from an option id. The comparison would therefore have read "changed" on the first save of most products and fired the very rebuild it was added to prevent. Worse, the same draft gated the write of `product_attribute` on that comparison, which would have made the "Visible on the product page" switch unsavable (that column is its only store) and would have permanently locked out enabling variations on a simple product, whose column is `''`. None of that is visible from the happy path; it came out of the refutation. A set-difference guard on `product_variants.variant` was then built instead, and **it too was refuted, this time in my own work** — it could not change the delete's outcome, because `whereNotIn('variant', $com)` already removes exactly the rows in `array_diff(existing, posted)`. It is recorded as F113 and removed. So the delete is **unchanged**, and what protects an ordinary save is (a) the non-destructive writer, (b) the values panel being built FROM the variant rows, so an ordinary save posts the set that already exists and the delete matches nothing, and (c) the pre-existing `ss_last_seen_at` conflict guard. The gate above still opens — that is unchanged — but it can no longer damage anything. **The owner's literal case is tested, and so is the reason it would otherwise pass for the wrong reason:** twelve variants with non-zero prices saved without touching a field. The panel echoes each stored price back, so a test asserting only prices, quantities and the row count **passes against the unfixed writer**. The assertions that bite are the columns the panel cannot echo — `sku` (rewritten unconditionally from the product name, so a hand-typed `SKU-0001` became `Scarf-S-Red` on every save), and `weight` / `stock_order_status` / `stock_status`, which are NULL and were written 0 or `''`. Four of the six tests in `ProductVariantOrdinarySaveGuardTest` fail when the controller is reverted; the two that do not are the deliberate guard rails (the configuration path still rebuilds, and the template premise). The two old tests that PINNED the defective behaviour were converted rather than deleted, per the owner's instruction. |
| **F106** | **The stock report's status column was empty on two of its three tabs, its row actions had no accessible name at all, and its active tab pill measured 2.59:1.** Three defects on one screen, all measured live. **(a) THE STATUS COLUMN.** Under a header reading "Stock status", the low-stock tab showed **nothing** on all five rows and the most-stocked tab nothing on all ten; only the out-of-stock tab rendered anything, a raw Bootstrap `bg-light-danger` pill. The partials gated on `stock_order_status` being one of four values — and `stock_order_status` is the merchant's **backorder policy** (allow / not_allow), not a stock level. The dev products carry NULL, so nothing rendered; **had the value read `allow`, the LOW-STOCK report would have shown a badge reading "In stock" on every low row.** Same category error as the two predicates F104 corrected: the setting means one thing and the screen said another. **(b) THE ROW ACTIONS had no accessible name, for a worse reason than F101's.** The `title` sat on the inner `<i>`, where it can never name the parent link, and Bootstrap then moved it to `data-bs-original-title` and blanked it. Measured: 5 links, accessible name empty on all 5, with `data-bs-original-title="تحرير"` sitting uselessly on the icon. Classes were `btn btn-sm btn-info`, which the order list recorded at 1.09:1 in dark. **(c) THE ACTIVE TAB PILL measured 2.59:1** — white on the host brand green `#10B77F` — in all four configurations, against the 4.5:1 text minimum. | **ALL THREE FIXED [2026-10-26].** The state is computed once by `ReportController::withStockState()` using **`ProductDataTable::trackedStockState()`** — the same function the product list's badge and the list's own filter use — and handed to the view as `stock_state`, so the three tabs cannot disagree with the catalogue. The partials render `DsBadge` with the matching tone and the rule in the title; measured live: low → `ss-badge--warning` "مخزون منخفض", out → `ss-badge--danger`, most-stocked → `ss-badge--success`. `stock_status` stays in the payload untouched because the datatables column definitions still reference it — no functionality removed. The actions are now `ss-btn ss-btn--sm ss-btn--icon ss-btn--ghost` with an `aria-label` naming the **product**, `title` on the link rather than the icon, and `aria-hidden` on the icon; measured: 5 of 5 named, e.g. "تعديل Hand-loomed Table Runner". `StockReportPredicateTest` grew to 9 tests pinning the badge tone AND label per tab (both halves — the right label present and the two wrong ones absent, because a view rendering every tone would satisfy a presence-only check) and the action's name, DS class and hidden icon. **THE TAB FIX IS DELIBERATELY SCOPED, and the reasoning is the interesting part.** The rail markup is shared by **four** views — `reports/stock_report`, `module/index`, `country/index`, `language/index` — and three belong to other slices. This file's own precedent (F76) is that a surface shared across slices is *recorded* rather than changed from inside one slice; but F76 was a dark-mode cosmetic and this is an accessibility failure P2-05-3's acceptance names, so the resolution is both: a new `ss-tabrail` class on this screen's rail only, styled with the existing `--ss-green-700` primitive (white on it measures **5.36:1**), and the other three screens recorded here as carrying the same 2.59:1 failure, unfixed, so they are not silently left behind. The host's brand green is untouched — darkening it globally would restyle the whole product for one pill's text. **AND THE MEASUREMENT ONLY HAPPENED BECAUSE A CLAIM WAS CHECKED:** adding `/stock-report` to the contrast harness's page list produced **zero** readings for it, because that harness keeps one reading per (config, element) and the first screen wins — so a new page contributes nothing unless it introduces new element labels. The first "64 measured, 0 below AA" run did not cover this screen at all. |
| **F107** | **The stock report's row actions were NAMED but NOT REACHABLE — the whole column sat outside the visible box at the starting scroll position, on all three tabs and in all eight configurations.** Found by looking at the screenshot taken as F106's evidence and noticing the Action column was not in it, then measured: the table is **905px** against an **803px** wrapper at 1440 and a **320px** one at 390, and the action column is **99px** — so 102px (or 585px) is hidden behind a horizontal scroll and the actions are exactly what is in it. A real click at the anchor's centre at scroll 0 hit something else in all eight configurations. **THIS IS WHY "the action has an accessible name" IS NOT THE SAME CLAIM AS "the merchant can use it".** F106 fixed the name and the fix was real; the control was still unusable, and the test that proved the name passes either way because it reads the rendered HTML, not the layout. | **FIXED [2026-10-26].** The same mechanism P2-03-1 proved on the product list, reused rather than reinvented: `ss-col-pinned` on each of the three action columns and `ss-dt-card-body` on each of the three card bodies — the second is load-bearing, because the CSS is scoped to `.ss-dt-card-body table.dataTable ... td.ss-col-pinned` and also supplies `--ss-dt-card-bg`, without which the sticky cell would scroll content under a transparent background. Measured after: the anchor is inside the wrapper and a real click **navigates** in all eight configurations. `StockReportPredicateTest::test_every_stock_tab_pins_its_action_column_to_the_visible_edge` asserts three pinned columns, three card bodies and the existence of the rule they depend on, so a fourth tab added later without it fails, and so does a rename of the CSS. |
| **F108** | **The two variant panels post INCOMPATIBLE SKU tokens into one writer, so every deliberate configuration change doubles the product name in every variant SKU.** The on-load panel posts `product_sku_X = '-' . $variant` (`attribute_combinations_data.blade.php:101`) while the post-interaction panel posts the FULL computed SKU, `$product_name . '-' . ...` (`attribute_combinations_edit.blade.php:9-18, :128`). The writer prefixes the product name to whatever it receives, so the first yields `Scarf-S-Red` and the second yields **`ScarfScarf-S-Red`**. | **RECORDED, NOT FIXED.** The D-09 change makes an ordinary save stop rewriting the SKU at all (an existing row keeps it unless the merchant typed a token differing from the panel's echo), which removes the common case. The doubling on the configuration path is **today's behaviour, unchanged** — fixing it means deciding what a variant SKU should be when the product is renamed, which is a merchant-facing naming rule, not a defect correction. Needs its own slice. |
| **F109** | **The attribute term resolver is UNSCOPED and silently DROPS what it cannot resolve, so an ordinary save can permanently shrink a product's stored configuration.** `ProductController.php:1812` (and the twin at `:1719`) is `ProductAttributeOption::where('terms', $fValue)->first()` — no `attribute_id` and no `store_id`. `ProductAttributeOption` is **not** in `config/tenancy.php` `scoped_models`, so the lookup crosses tenants, and `product_attribute_options` has no unique index on `(attribute_id, terms)`, so two attributes may legitimately share a term and the globally-first row wins. Then `if ($option) { $optionValues[] = $option->id; }` — a term whose option row was deleted is dropped with no error, and `:1835` writes the shrunken set back. | **OPEN.** Deliberately out of the D-09 change, which is about `product_variants`: this is `products.product_attribute`, and the obvious fix (skip the write when a term fails to resolve) interacts with the two lockouts the D-09 refutation found in exactly that area. It needs its own evidence — in particular whether duplicate `(attribute_id, terms)` rows exist in production, which cannot be answered from this repository. |
| **F110** | **`$is_in_stock` can never be true, and the column it would have written does not exist.** `ProductController.php:2152` is `if ($product_stock->stock == 'in_stock')` — `product_variants.stock` is an **integer** column, and on PHP 8 `0 == 'in_stock'` is `false` (executed), as is any other integer. So `$is_in_stock` stays false and `:2198` always runs `$product->stock = 'out_of_stock'`. **`products` has no `stock` column at all** (its stock columns are `product_stock`, `stock_status`, `stock_order_status`, `low_stock_threshold`, `track_stock`), and the last `$product->save()` in `update()` is at `:1991`, *before* that line — so the assignment is dead twice over and persists nothing. The same shape exists in `store()` at `:1051`/`:1055`. | **RECORDED, NOT FIXED.** It writes nothing today, so correcting the comparison would start writing something — a change to what a save does to stock, which is reserved. What the merchant actually sees comes from `stock_status` and `product_stock`, which are written elsewhere. Worth deleting as dead code in a cleanup slice, with the comparison's intent re-derived rather than guessed. |
| **F111** | **The "Used for variations" switch is wired for the FIRST attribute only.** `edit.blade.php:983-992` and `:1003-1013` derive `dataid` from the first `.attribute_option_data` and bind `$('.enable_variation_' + dataid).on('change', ...)`, so toggling the SECOND attribute's switch never calls `update_attribute()` and the values panel is never rebuilt for it. | **RECORDED, NOT FIXED.** Pre-existing and independent of D-09. Note it is *why* the D-09 guard loses nothing on that path: with the panel never refreshed, the posted combination set is the one that already exists, so the old code's "rebuild" rewrote the same rows — it never produced the new Cartesian combinations either. |
| **F112** | **`$item` is never reset inside the attribute loops, so the second element of the stored `product_attribute` inherits the first's flags.** Four copies of the same loop (`:341`, `:657`, `:1752`, `:1846`) assign `$item['attribute_id']`, `$item['values']`, `$item['visible_attribute_N']` and `$item['for_variation_N']` without `$item = []` first, and the flag keys are **suffixed by attribute id**, so element 2 of the JSON carries element 1's `visible_attribute_1` and `for_variation_1` alongside its own. Harmless to the readers that look up their own suffix; it is why the stored shape cannot be compared naively, and it is one of the reasons the D-09 guard does not read that column. | **RECORDED, NOT FIXED.** A one-line change in four places, but it changes the stored shape of a column three other code paths parse, so it needs its own slice with the readers checked. |
| **F113** | **Both guards added to the variant delete in the first D-09 commit were defective, and independent review of my own change is what found them.** **(a) THE SET-DIFFERENCE GATE WAS A NO-OP.** It computed `array_diff(existing, posted)` and ran the delete only when the sets differed — but `whereNotIn('variant', $com)` already deletes exactly the rows in that diff, so the gate is a restatement of "this delete has something to delete" and can never change an outcome. It also read the existing set **after** the upsert loop, which guarantees `posted ⊆ existing` and made the second half of the comparison dead code. A guard that cannot change an outcome cannot be tested by outcomes, and the docblock above it claimed a protection that was not there — that is the defect, not the wasted query. **(b) `->where('store_id', getCurrentStore())` ON THE DELETE WAS A REGRESSION.** It bought nothing — the delete is already confined by `product_id` and the product has passed `update()`'s operator-first ownership check, so no caller can reach another store's variants through it — and it cost the **platform operator's** cleanup entirely: `getCurrentStore()` returns the literal **1** for an admin with no `current_store` (`helper.php:636`), so an operator editing a merchant's product would match no row, and every combination they removed would stay live and purchasable on that merchant's storefront (`HomeController`'s price range and the cart's variant check both read variants without a store filter). It would also have spared any row whose `store_id` is NULL, which `product_variants` permits and which the `choice_no` path creates. | **BOTH REMOVED [2026-10-26]**, before the work was committed, and the reasoning left in the code at the delete so the next person does not re-add them. The D-09 fix is therefore the **writer** alone, which is what the behavioural tests prove: six of twelve tests across the two variant files fail when the controller is reverted. `ProductVariantPersistenceTest::test_every_per_variant_column_is_written_through_the_non_destructive_helper` replaced the test that pinned the removed gate, and now pins the thing that actually protects — every per-variant column going through `ssVariantValue()`, with no `!empty($request[` left in the writer. **The lesson is the one this ledger keeps recording:** the first design was refuted by review, its replacement was refuted by review of the replacement, and in both cases the error was invisible from the happy path. |
| **F114** | **A variant's `shipping_id_*` is stored from caller input with no ownership check, so another store's shipping row id can be written onto a variant and priced against.** `ProductController.php` writes `$product_stock->shipping` straight from the posted select value; `Shipping` is not in `scoped_models` and nothing checks that the id belongs to the caller's store. Found during the security review of the D-09 change, on a line that change rewrote. | **RECORDED, NOT FIXED — pre-existing and out of D-09's scope.** The D-09 change preserved the previous semantics exactly, including the blank-means-"same as parent" mapping; it neither widened nor narrowed what can be stored. Fixing it means validating the id against the store's own shipping rows, which is a shipping-slice change with its own tests. Worth doing before P2-04 touches fulfilment. |
| **F115** | **The plan-activation replay guard keys on a TRANSLATED STRING, so a locale switch between two arrivals defeats it — and this is the existing guard, not new code.** `GuardsPlanActivation::existingPlanOrderForProviderTxn(string $paymentType, $txnId)` matches `plan_orders` on `payment_type` + `txn_id`. Both of its callers build that first argument with `__()`: `SenangPayController.php:166` (`$paymentType = __('SenangPay')`) and `OzowController.php` likewise. **Measured by execution**, not assumed: under `ar` the application returns `__('SenangPay') = 'سينانغ باي'`, `__('Ozow') = 'أوزو'` and `__('Paystack') = 'كومة الدفع'`. So a plan order written while the merchant's locale was Arabic is **invisible** to a replay check made while the locale is English, and the guard silently finds nothing — in ordinary mixed-locale use, not only under an attack. This platform is bilingual by design and the admin app ships in both, so the two spellings genuinely coexist. | **FIXED FOR PAYSTACK ONLY [2026-10-26], RECORDED FOR THE OTHER TWO.** The D-12 work could not build a correct replay guard without resolving this, so `PaystackPaymentController::PAYMENT_TYPE` is a locale-independent `'Paystack'` used for **both** the lookup and the stored value, with the translated spelling checked as a fallback so rows already written under the current locale are still found. `PaystackPlanActivationTest::test_the_replay_key_is_not_locale_dependent` proves the translation exists and that the constant escapes it. **SenangPay and Ozow are NOT fixed**: changing their stored `payment_type` changes what is written for every future row while existing rows keep the old spelling, which is a data decision, not a patch. The residue even for Paystack is a row written under a THIRD locale, which neither spelling finds. |
| **F116** | **`plan_orders.payment_type` holds a translated string while three super-admin surfaces compare it against English literals, so a bank-transfer plan order created in Arabic loses its approve action and its invoice link.** `BankTransferController.php:103` writes `$order->payment_type = __('Bank Transfer')`, which under `ar` is `تحويل مصرفي` (executed). `PlanOrderDataTable` renders `plans/action.blade.php` and `plans/receipt.blade.php`, and those gate on literals: `action.blade.php:2` `$order->payment_status == 'Pending' && $order->payment_type == 'Bank Transfer'` — the button that opens the **change-payment-status** dialog — and `receipt.blade.php:2,7,9` on `'STRIPE'`, `'Manually'` and `'Bank Transfer'` for the invoice link. `__('STRIPE')` is `الشريط` under `ar`. **So a merchant who buys a plan by bank transfer while using the product in Arabic produces a row the super admin cannot approve from the list**, and whose invoice link does not render. Same root cause as F115, different consequence: F115 is a security guard missing, this is a function disappearing. | **RECORDED, NOT FIXED — needs a data decision, and every cheap patch is wrong.** Comparing against `__('Bank Transfer')` in the blade does not fix it: a row written in Arabic and viewed in English still misses, because the comparison follows the VIEWER's locale and the data followed the WRITER's. Storing canonical literals going forward (as Paystack now does) fixes new rows and leaves existing ones broken, so it needs a backfill of `plan_orders.payment_type` — **production data, which the standing limits reserve**. Recorded with the measurement so the decision is taken on evidence. Scope note: this is Phase 06 (SaaS and super admin) territory; it is recorded here because D-12 is what uncovered it. |
| **F117** | **Sixteen credential-bearing fields across eleven settings panels render their stored value into the page, and only one credential field in the whole settings tree is masked.** Measured: `mail_password`, `twilio_sid`, `twilio_token`, `whatsapp_access_token` (twice), `s3_secret`, `wasabi_secret`, `shopify_access_token`, `woocommerce_consumer_secret`, `google_recaptcha_secret`, `mail_encryption` (three times), the ChatGPT `api_key[]`, and — the one a careless scan misses — `delivery_syria_token` at `setting/index.blade.php:251`, which is `value="{{auth()->user()->deliverysyria_secret}}"`, a per-merchant courier secret. Every one is `type="text"` or `type="hidden"`; not one of the sixteen is `type="password"`. This is the acceptance condition P2-05-7 and P2-05-8 both carry — "no secret or credential is rendered into the page" — and it does not hold anywhere. **THE FIRST MEASUREMENT SAID FOURTEEN AND WAS WRONG**, which is worth recording because the error is systematic rather than careless: the scanner matched `<input\b[^>]*>`, and a `>` inside a Blade expression (`{{ $key_data->key }}`, `{{auth()->user()->…}}`) ends that match early, so the `value` attribute was never seen. A scanner that stops at the first `>` under-reports exactly the fields whose values come from an object — which is most of them. The committed scanner is quote-aware and asserts that property about itself. | **RECORDED AND RATCHETED [2026-10-26], NOT FIXED — and the fix is mechanical, not a design question.** `tests/Unit/Ui/SettingsCredentialExposureTest.php` (4 tests) pins the sixteen so the set cannot grow silently, and pins the premise that the scan reaches the views. **THE PATTERN TO COPY IS ALREADY IN THE REPOSITORY, ONCE**, and the test found it by refuting my own blanket claim that nothing was masked: `setting/otp_setting.blade.php:34` renders `api_secret` as `type="password"`, `autocomplete="new-password"`, **no `value` attribute**, placeholder *"Leave empty to keep current secret"* — and `OtpIntegrationController:40` closes the loop with `if ($request->filled('api_secret')) { … }`. **BOTH HALVES ARE THE PATTERN AND MUST LAND TOGETHER.** Masking alone would be far worse than the exposure: these panels post every field on every save, so an empty box with an unchanged save path would erase the merchant's SMTP password, Twilio token and S3 secret the first time anyone pressed Save on an unrelated setting. The ratchet asserts the precedent as a pair — the masked view AND the `filled()` guard — so it cannot be half-copied. This is the body of P2-05-7 / P2-05-8. **[2026-10-27] CLOSED — see the F117 row below.** All sixteen are converted, the ratchet list is EMPTY, and the three raw `type="password"` inputs that remain are documented exceptions (the OTP precedent, and the two JavaScript string templates the ChatGPT add-a-row button clones, which Blade never renders). |
| **F118** | **The shipped Flutter admin app's own order writer decrements stock with a check-then-act read-modify-write, and every stock census in this project missed it.** `Api\DashboardController::createOrderViaApi` — routed at `routes/api.php:240` as `POST api/admin/createOrderViaApi` with `custom.auth`, i.e. **the admin app's order writer, the most important one in Phase 02** — decrements at `:5834` (`$variant->stock -= $qty; $variant->save()`) and `:5860` (`$product->product_stock -= $qty; $product->save()`). `grep -c` for `ProductStockService|AppliesCartStockChanges` in that file returns **0**. **WHY EVERY CENSUS MISSED IT, which is the durable half:** the surveys were keyed on the `$remain_stock = ... - $qtyy` spelling that the seven gateway controllers share. This writer spells the same operation `-=`, so a grep-shaped census reports a complete picture and is silent about the one writer a Phase 02 reader would most want to know about. **THE DEFECT IS NARROWER THAN THE GATEWAY ONES, and saying so matters:** this handler runs inside `\DB::beginTransaction()` with `\DB::rollBack()` on every error exit, and it REFUSES a shortfall (`if ($variant->stock < $qty) { rollBack; return 400; }`) rather than overselling. So the decrement is reversible and guarded — what it is not is **atomic**: the read and the write are separate statements, so two concurrent orders can both observe `stock >= qty` and both decrement, which is exactly the lost update `ProductStockService`'s conditional UPDATE exists to prevent. | **RECORDED, NOT FIXED.** Two reasons to keep it out of the D-13 work rather than fold it in. (1) **Authority:** D-18 approves routing through `ProductStockService` for Khalti, PaiementPro, CinetPay and Easebuzz by name; this writer is not among them, and it is an API the Flutter app depends on, so changing what it does to stock needs its own decision. (2) **It is a different change:** the gateway work is "make an unguarded decrement accounted for and reversible"; here the decrement is already both, and the fix is "make the availability test and the decrement one atomic statement", which alters the refusal's timing and therefore the response the app receives. **It is also the writer that most needs E13's durable movement record**, and E13's design is written on the assumption that enrolling the gateway writers achieves coverage — it does not, while this one is outside the set. |
| **F119** | **`plan_orders` has no unique index on `(payment_type, txn_id)`, so every plan-activation replay guard in the codebase is a check-then-act with no database backstop.** Measured from the live schema: the table carries `plan_orders_order_id_unique` and `plan_orders_store_id_index` and nothing else. `order_id` being unique does not help, because each request generates a fresh one (`uniqid()`), so two concurrent arrivals with the SAME provider reference both pass `existingPlanOrderForProviderTxn()`, both insert, and both activate a plan. The window is small — it needs two arrivals inside one lookup-to-insert gap — but it is the only thing between the guard and a double activation, and it applies to SenangPay, Ozow and now Paystack alike. | **RECORDED, NOT FIXED — it needs a migration, which is production data and reserved.** A unique index on `(payment_type, txn_id)` is the right shape, but it cannot be added blind: existing rows may already violate it (every provider that recorded `time()` or a blank as `txn_id` would collide), so it needs a duplicate census on the real database first. That census cannot be run from here. Note the interaction with **F115/F116**: `payment_type` currently holds a translated string, so a unique index over it would treat `Paystack` and `كومة الدفع` as different providers — the canonicalisation has to come first or the index would enforce the wrong thing. |
| **F120** | **Closing F87's "a failed payment activates the plan" half left its cheaper half wide open: a real payment for the cheapest plan activates the most expensive one.** Found by the independent review OF MY OWN FIX, not by the fix. `status === 'success'` plus a matching `data.reference` establish that *some* transaction in the platform's Paystack account succeeded. They establish nothing about **which plan** it bought. The plan is a route parameter — and the encrypted id does not even need forging, because `plans/stripe.blade.php:3246` prints `{{ encrypt($plan->id) }}` into the callback URL for **every plan card on the page**. So a merchant genuinely buys the cheapest paid plan, and instead of letting the JS callback send them to `/plan-paystack-payment/<ref>/<encrypt(cheap)>`, opens `/plan-paystack-payment/<ref>/<encrypt(expensive)>` once. First use, so the replay guard does not fire; the reference verifies; the expensive plan is assigned, and the row records the expensive `plan_id` beside the cheap captured `price`. **The amount was the only field that could tell those apart, and the first fix LOGGED it.** | **FIXED [2026-10-26] in the same change, and the reasoning that had excused it is the part worth keeping.** The first version declined to refuse on amount, citing `GuardsPlanActivation`'s own warning that a blind comparison "would refuse real payments on a live integration" because a provider may report minor units — and concluding this repo only *believed* Paystack did, since it divides by 100. **The evidence was already in the repository and the review found it: this server renders the checkout**, and sends `amount: res.total_price * 100` (`stripe.blade.php:3228`) with `currency: res.currency`. Both sides are first-party and agree; were it otherwise, every Paystack plan sale already recorded would sit at a hundredth of its price. The comparison is now a refusal, done in minor units on both sides so no rounding is introduced, with currency refused only when both values are non-empty and differ (a platform with no configured currency must not have every purchase refused). **A prerequisite had to be fixed first or the refusal would have rejected every discounted purchase:** the callback URL carries `?coupon_id=`, an **id**, while `serverOwnedPlanPrice()` resolves a coupon by **code**, so the server price was silently always the undiscounted one. Harmless while it only logged; fatal as a gate. **The general lesson is the one this ledger keeps recording:** the first fix was reasoned, documented and wrong in the same breath — the docblock explaining why the amount could not be trusted was the thing that stopped anyone looking for the proof, which was two files away. |
| **F121** | **The four gateway return handlers carry "Product not found" exits inside their stock loops that cannot be reached, because `Cart::cart_list_cookie()` dereferences the same missing rows earlier in the request and raises a 500 first.** Found while trying to write a behavioural test for D-13's restoration: a basket whose second line names a product id with no row should decrement line one, then take the loop's `return $this->error(['message' => 'Product not found .'])` exit. **Driven, it produced an HTTP 500**, from `ErrorException: Attempt to read property "id" on null` at `app/Models/Cart.php:531`, called at `PaiementProPaymentController.php:495` — i.e. in the price-computation stage, long before the loop. The variant form of the same fixture crashes two lines further on at `Cart.php:537` (`ProductVariant::find($variant_id)` then `->variant`), and that unguarded shape repeats at roughly a dozen sites in `Cart.php`, so the authenticated path has it too. **`GuardsGatewayReturn::basketHasForeignProduct()` deliberately lets these lines through** — its own comment says "A variant id that resolves to NO row is left alone … every one of these loops already has a 'Product not found' path for it" — so the design intends the loop to handle it, and the loop never gets the chance. | **RECORDED, NOT FIXED, AND IT CHANGED WHAT D-13 CLAIMS.** The restore calls D-13 added at those three exits are therefore **defensive rather than active** on the paths that could be driven here, and `GatewayStockDecrementRoutingTest`'s docblock says so rather than claiming a basket gives its stock back. **A behavioural test asserting the restoration was written, passed, and was DELETED** when the revert check showed it passing against the unfixed handlers too: it could not tell "failed inside the loop after decrementing" from "crashed before the loop", because its premise only checked that no order was written. That is the vacuity trap this project keeps paying for, caught this time by the discipline rather than by a reviewer. **The crash itself is the real defect here** — a cart line whose product was deleted between checkout and return 500s the gateway return for the shopper — and fixing it means null-guarding `Cart.php`'s product and variant lookups, which is a change to the checkout pricing path and belongs in its own slice. **OWNER APPROVED ITS OWN SLICE [2026-10-27] — D-22.** Both halves named: a missing row must refuse cleanly BEFORE the decrement, and must NOT refuse after the capture, because the customer has paid — "بحيث لا يضيع طلب مدفوع أو يُطلب من العميل الدفع مجدداً". And a direct instruction on method: "لا تعتمد على اختبارات تمر حتى بعد التراجع عن الإصلاح". |
| **F122** | **An order does not record WHICH VARIANT it contained, so "this variant is safe to delete" cannot be proven — and that measurement is what shaped D-20's implementation.** Only `carts` and `wishlists` carry a `variant_id` **column**. An order records its contents in `orders.product_json`, and the storefront writer's line shape has **no `variant_id` key at all**: measured across the development fixture, **all 14 order lines in all 7 orders** carry exactly `final_price, name, price, product_id, qty, total_orignal_price` — and **zero** carry a non-empty variant id. Other writers do emit one (`Order.php:310`, `:323` set `variant_id`, and `:899`/`:912` read it back), so the column-in-JSON exists in some shapes and not others; which means a search of order history can find SOME references and can never establish their ABSENCE. | **RECORDED, AND IT DECIDED THE DESIGN RATHER THAN BLOCKING IT.** D-20 condition 4 asks for the references that make a delete dangerous to be checked; condition 5 says an unproven delete must be refused. Since order references are **unprovable**, the implementation does both: a reference it CAN see (a cart row, a wishlist row, or an order line that does carry the id) is a **hard refusal** that confirmation cannot override, and everything else is refused until the merchant confirms explicitly — with the refusal stating plainly that order history may still refer to the variants and that this cannot be checked. **The alternative would have been to search `product_json` and report "no references found", which would have been true and misleading.** The deeper fix — recording the variant on every order line — is order-schema work the owner's D-20 scope limit explicitly excludes ("لا تغيّر آلية الطلبات"). **OWNER CORRECTION [2026-10-27]: CONFIRMATION IS NOT PROOF.** The first implementation let an explicit confirmation carry a delete whose safety was unprovable. The owner refused that trade — "التأكيد شرط للتغييرات المسموحة، لكنه لا يلغي شرط إثبات السلامة" — so an unverifiable order history is now a **hard block** that ignores the confirmation flag. **What makes a PERMITTED case possible is the other half of the same measurement:** `product_id` is on **14 of 14** order lines while `variant_id` is on **0 of 14**, so "no order ever named this PRODUCT" IS establishable and entails that no order named any of its variants. That is the only case where a confirmed delete now proceeds. **The no-loss alternative the owner asked about was measured and NOT built:** `stock_status = 'out_of_stock'` blocks the cart add for a variant that is not stock-managed (`Cart.php:793` admits only `in_stock`/`on_backorder`), but for a stock-managed variant `stock_status` is not consulted at all and the only lever is `stock = 0` — two different levers chosen by a flag inside a comma-separated string, with the storefront API and vendored module cart routes untraced. Building a merchant-facing retire action on that footing would be the same unproven safety the owner just refused. **D-20 is PARTIAL.** |
| **F123** | **The D-21 stock conversion shipped with two post-capture exits that took stock and gave nothing back — a defect introduced BY the fix, in the fix.** `AuthorizeNetPaymnetController`'s product loop has **five** in-loop error exits, not three. The first pass located them with a detector keyed on `return $this->error(`, so it never saw the two spelled `return redirect()->back()->with('error', ...)`: the foreign-product refusal (`:778`) and the missing-variant refusal (`:818`). Both sit **after the Authorize.Net capture**, so each left the shopper charged, no order row written, and every basket line already processed decremented with nothing returned. **CI could not see it:** the ratchet asserted `assertGreaterThanOrEqual($loops, substr_count(..., 'restoreStockDecrements'))` — a floor of one restore per loop — and the three exits that did restore satisfied it. Found by the independent security review of the change, which counted the exits instead of matching a pattern. | **FIXED, AND THE RATCHET REPLACED WITH THE PROPERTY IT SHOULD HAVE ASSERTED.** Both restores added. `GatewayStockDecrementRoutingTest::test_each_authorised_handler_gives_back_what_it_took_on_every_in_loop_exit` no longer counts occurrences: it locates each product loop by brace depth and asserts a restore stands immediately before **every** `return` inside it, over comment-stripped lines that keep their numbering (`AuthorizeNet:727` contains the literal `return $this->error(...)` inside a block comment). **It carries no list of exit spellings, and that was earned:** the first replacement did list four, and promptly reported `EasebuzzController` as having no exits at all — that handler returns `Utility::error(...)`. A census found **30 returns inside a stock-taking loop across all seven handlers and all 30 are error exits**, so the rule applies to every `return`; a legitimate value return added later fails the test and earns a named exception rather than a pattern that quietly stops matching. **Revert-checked:** without the two restores the test fails naming line 776; with them, 4 tests / 252 assertions pass. Two further findings recorded in `D-21-STOCK-WRITER-INVENTORY.md` §4 rather than fixed here — **PayU and `Api/ApiController` have no `basketHasUnusableQuantity` pre-flight**, so for them the conversion closes a real stock-**inflation** path (a negative qty made `$stock - $qtyy` add stock; `normaliseQuantity()` now returns null and the service SKIPs), which means the change is not inert on those two and is not claimed to be; and **`PayUPaymentController:507` dereferences `$ProductStock->variation_option` before `:509`'s null test**, so its restore at `:605` is unreachable — the same post-capture 500 as F121, left to **D-22** rather than widened into this slice. |
| **F124** | **The Flutter admin writer's conversion is invisible to every behavioural test of it — measured, not suspected.** Reverting `Api/DashboardController::createOrderViaApi` to its original check-then-act (`if ($variant->stock < $qty) { rollBack; 400 }` then `$variant->stock -= $qty; save()`) leaves **all four** tests in `AdminApiStockRefusalContractTest` passing: the shortfall still answers 400 with the same envelope, the boundary case still succeeds, the happy path still takes the stock. That is by design — the owner's D-21 Flutter condition requires the shipped app to see no change, above all no new 401 — but it means behaviour is the wrong place to look for evidence that the conversion happened, and a suite that only drives behaviour would have reported a reverted fix as green. | **RATCHETED IN SOURCE.** `test_create_order_via_api_delegates_stock_to_the_service_and_keeps_no_read_modify_write` pins it over the comment-stripped body of that one method (`ReflectionMethod`, precedent `OrderStoreOwnershipTest:263-290`): both branches must call the service, **both must pass `$requireAvailability = true`** — without it the service oversells instead of refusing, turning the pinned 400 into a 200 with an order written against stock that is not there — and the four spellings of the read-modify-write must not return. **Revert-checked: the conversion reverted fails that test and only that test.** What it does NOT prove is stated in the file: converting a writer to `ProductStockService` is not on its own evidence that the path is protected (the owner's instruction is explicit), and the lost-update property the conversion buys cannot be driven in this suite at all — SQLite `:memory:` with `CACHE_STORE=array` makes `Cache::lock` process-local, so no Feature test can stage two overlapping requests. That property is proven against the service by `OrderStockConcurrencyTest`; this test proves only the thing that would silently undo it. |

Checked before changing it: all eight consumers use `{!! !!}` and none re-escapes, so none now double-encodes — confirmed in the running application, where `Scarves & Shawls` renders with a literal `&`. `Tests\Unit\Security\CategoryTreeEscapingTest` (4 tests) pins both halves at once: the name is escaped AND the indent stays live markup, because a fix that satisfies only the first breaks the hierarchy the select exists to show. **Revert-checked:** 3 of the 4 fail on the unfixed helper; the indent test passes before and after, which is what makes it a guard rather than a restatement. |

The host's Bootstrap modal keeps a **white** `.modal-content` in dark mode (measured `rgb(255, 255, 255)` with `data-ss-theme="dark"` on `<html>`), so `#9DA3B4` on it is **2.52:1** against the 4.5:1 body text needs. **It does NOT originate in the design-system conversion**, and that was checked rather than assumed: the previous markup used `<label class="form-label">`, still a `label`, still matched — six of them on the create-category form alone. A SECOND cause compounded it: the design system's light palette lived on `:root` alone, so although the dark block is documented as working "on `<html>` or on any container", a subtree inside a dark page could never be returned to light. The six modal forms this slice converts declare `data-ss-theme="light"`, which is not a preference but a statement of the surface the host actually paints. Every piece of text in the create-category modal now clears AA in all four configurations, measured over the **effective** background with alpha compositing. **NOT FIXED:** every other modal in the admin — roughly sixty forms across the host and seventy vendored modules — still renders its labels at 2.52:1, because the bare `body label` rule still reaches them.
